h_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{8}{3}+\frac{9}{16}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0-5120q+668160\mbox{q}^{2}}+112032256\mbox{q}^{3}+2015342615552\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&+1027768507417600\mbox{q}^{5}+184583137843579904\mbox{q}^{6}+17979440506308718592\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{2}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{8}{3}+\frac{1}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+0\mbox{q}+276864\mbox{q}^{2}}-32485376\mbox{q}^{3}+176489687424\mbox{q}^{4}+168522803580928\mbox{q}^{5}\\
&+39373360484128256\mbox{q}^{6}+4527688807584194560\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{3}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{8}{3}+\frac{1}{16}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+0\mbox{q}+7680\mbox{q}^{2}}-32203776\mbox{q}^{3}+27746555904\mbox{q}^{4}+53778203675136\mbox{q}^{5}\\
&+15108125739695104\mbox{q}^{6}+1937976067726382592\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{4}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{8}{3}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+\underline{12}\mbox{q}-\underline{2112}\mbox{q}^{2}}-34689216\mbox{q}^{3}+10834429824\mbox{q}^{4}+36099879476640\mbox{q}^{5}\\
&+10900431340916352\mbox{q}^{6}+1454331023779312896\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

Furthermore, coefficients of
\mbox{q}^{2}
,
\mbox{q}^{3}
in
h_{0}
,
\mbox{q}^{2}
,
\mbox{q}^{3}
,
\mbox{q}^{4}
in
h_{1}
, up to
\mbox{q}^{5}
in
h_{2}
,
\mbox{q}^{3}
,
\mbox{q}^{4}
,
\mbox{q}^{5}
in
h_{3}
, and
\mbox{q}^{4}
in
h_{4}
are also reproduced by (
4.19
) with
k=k_{0}-1
. Thus, there is overwhelming evidence that (
5.14
) is correct.
While the maximal genus attainable by the standard direct integration method is 50, using modularity we can predict
GV invariants close to the Castelnuovo bound to arbitrary genus (see Table
5
), and provide sufficiently many boundary conditions
to push the
direct integration method up to genus 64.

6.
Discussion

In this work, we have exploited a triangle of relations between Gopakumar-Vafa invariants
{{\rm GV}}^{(g)}_{Q}
, which determine the topological string partition function on a CY threefold
\mathfrak{Y}
, Pandharipande-Thomas invariants
\operatorname{PT}(Q,m)
which count bound states of a single anti-D6-brane with
Q
D2 and
m
D0 branes, and D4-D2-D0 invariants
\overline{\Omega}_{r,\mu}(\hat{q}_{0})
, which count BPS black holes with
r
units of D4-brane charge along an ample divisor
\mathcal{D}
, and D2-D0 brane charge determined by
\mu
and
\hat{q}_{0}
. Mathematically, these invariants count embedded curves, stable pairs and Gieseker-stable coherent sheaves supported on
\mathcal{D}
, respectively.
While the relation between GV and PT invariants is standard
[
11
]
, and relations between GV invariants and D4-D2-D0 indices were first proposed in
[
15
]
, we used a novel explicit formula (
4.19
) proven in Appendix
A
, which applies for one-parameter CY threefolds with
\Pic\mathfrak{Y}=\mathds{Z}H
(or more generally, CY threefolds satisfying Assumption (*) in Prop.
2
) and for
r=1
unit of D4-brane charge. We applied this formula for the 13 CY threefolds of hypergeometric type, for which we have computed GV invariants (and therefore PT invariants) to relatively high genus using the direct integration method of
[
13
]
.

For most models, we could rigorously compute the first few coefficients in the generating series of Abelian D4-D2-D0 invariants, including both polar and non-polar terms, and find a unique vector-valued modular form which reproduces all of them, providing a
striking confirmation of the modularity properties predicted by string theory. These results also provide indirect support for the BMT inequality which is assumed in the derivation of (
4.19
), in cases where it is not yet known to hold.
For
X_{3,3}
,
X_{4,3}
,
X_{3,2,2}
and
X_{2,2,2,2}
, we could not compute sufficiently many terms rigorously to uniquely fix the vector-valued modular form, but in the first two cases we could determine a unique candidate which agrees with the formula (
4.19
) for many coefficients, albeit outside the known regime of validity for this formula.
For
X_{3,2,2}
and
X_{2,2,2,2}
, our current knowledge of GV invariants is not sufficient to identify the modular form with sufficient confidence.
Conversely, in cases where the vector-valued modular form could be identified,
we used these modular predictions
to determine GV invariants close to the Castelnuovo bound for arbitrarily high genus. These results provide new boundary conditions for the direct integration method, which in principle allow to reach higher genus than hitherto possible (in practice, some computational limitations need to be overcome in order to reach such high genera).
The case of
X_{4,3}
is particularly noteworthy, as it requires combining information from direct integration, modularity and wall-crossing to go beyond the restrictions imposed by each of these methods separately.

These results raise several natural questions. First, it is intriguing that the naive Ansatz (
4.8
), which was proposed as an educated guess in
[
31
]
, so often manages to produce the correct polar terms.
As discussed in §
4.1
, a similar result (
4.3
) arises by studying the chamber structure of rank 0 DT-invariants in the space of weak stability conditions.
Unfortunately, the walls can only be controlled under the very restrictive assumption (
4.2
) which in practice limits its use to the most polar terms. It would be very interesting to relax the condition (
4.2
), but this seems to require a stronger bound on
\ch_{3}
than provided by the standard BMT inequality. Physically, such a result would give a clear physical origin of the polar coefficients in terms of bound states of D6-
\overline{\rm D6}
-branes, as proposed in
[
16
]
. Instead, the formula (
4.12
) expresses the spectrum of anti-D6-branes as a sum of bound states of D4-D2-D0 branes and anti-D6-branes with lower D4-D2-D0 brane charge, and does not provide any insight on the micro-structure of D4-D2-D0 bound states by themselves.

A second question is, why on earth should the generating series of Abelian D4-D2-D0 invariants be modular. Of course, physics gives a clear reason, by identifying them with the elliptic genus of the
(0,4)
-superconformal field theory obtained by wrapping an M5-brane on the divisor
\mathcal{D}
. From the mathematics viewpoint however, modularity is still largely mysterious. For non-compact CY threefolds of the form
\mathfrak{Y}=K_{S}
where
S
is a complex projective surface, the generating series of Abelian D4-D2-D0 invariants supported on the divisor
S
is given by Göttsche’s formula for the Euler number of the Hilbert scheme of points on
S

[
91
]
, which is manifestly modular. For K3-fibered CY threefolds, the modularity of vertical D4-D2-D0 indices (counting D4-branes supported on a K3-fiber) can be shown to follow from Göttsche’s formula for the Hilbert scheme of points on
K3
and from the modularity of the generating series of Noether-Lefschetz numbers determined by the fibration
[
41
]
. In our generating series (
5.5
) of Abelian D4-D2-D0 invariants, it is tempting to identify the factor
1/\eta^{\kappa+c_{2}}
as coming from the Hilbert scheme of points on the divisor
\mathcal{D}
, and the remainder as the generating series of some Noether-Lefschetz-type numbers taking into account the moduli of the divisor
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathfrak{Y}
equipped with a line bundle
[
5
]
. Eventually, one would hope that modularity can be derived from the existence of an underlying vertex operator algebra acting on the cohomology of the moduli space of semi-stable sheaves, similar to the case of Hilbert scheme of points on surfaces
[
92
]
.

Third, it would be very interesting to generalize this approach to the case
of non-Abelian D4-D2-D0 indices, where the generating series are expected to be mock modular. While the relation between rank 1 and rank 0 DT invariants
from
[
8
, Thm 1.2]

covers this case, it requires taking
the spectral flow parameter to be large enough, with unspecified lower bound.
Nonetheless, we expect that the approach in Appendix
A
can be generalized and used to compute polar coefficients for
r>1
as well. The strategy outlined in
[
31
]
can then be used to construct a suitable mock modular series (using the generating series of Hurwitz class numbers to cancel the modular anomaly in the
r=2
case). In a subsequent work
[
93
]
, we apply this strategy
for the models
X_{8}
and
X_{10}
at rank
r=2
and verify the
mock modular properties predicted in
[
22
,
23
,
24
,
49
]
.
It would also be desirable to generalize this approach to other classes of one-parameter CY threefolds (such as freely acting orbifolds of hypergeometric models, or complete intersections in Grassmannians or Pfaffians), and to the
CY threefolds with 2 or more Kähler parameters. In particular, we expect an interesting interplay between the modularity of D4-D2-D0 invariants and the modularity associated to elliptic fibrations
[
94
,
95
,
96
]
.

Finally, having found the generating series of Abelian D4-D2-D0 invariants, it is now straightforward to extract the asymptotic growth of the Fourier coefficients, and produce a Rademacher-type series which computes them explicitly
[
97
,
98
,
99
,
21
]
. It would be very interesting to reproduce these microstate degeneracies from localization in supergravity, in analogy to cases with
\mathcal{N}=8
or
\mathcal{N}=4
supersymmetry
[
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
]
(see
[
105
,
106
,
107
]
for some progress in this direction).
We hope to return to these issues in near future.

Appendix A
New explicit formulae, by S. Feyzbakhsh

Let
(\mathfrak{Y},H)
be a smooth polarised Calabi-Yau threefold (i.e.
K_{\mathfrak{Y}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}
and
H^{1}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}})=0
) with
\Pic(\mathfrak{Y})={\mathbb{Z}}.H
satisfying the BMT conjecture. In §
A.1
, we improve the result of
[
8
, Theorem 1.2]
for rank zero classes with minimal D4-brane charge
\ch_{1}=H
and obtain, under some assumptions, an explicit formula for the stable pair invariants
\operatorname{PT}_{m,\beta}
in terms of rank zero DT invariants and stable pair invariants
\operatorname{PT}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
for
\beta^{\prime}.H<\beta.H
. In §
A.2
, we explain how this result can be inverted to determine rank 0 DT invariants with minimal D4-brane charge from stable pair invariants. In §
A.3
, we apply the wall-crossing argument for Theorem
1
to establish a Castelnuovo-type bound
for PT invariants, and compute the PT invariants saturating this bound explicitly for the quintic threefold. In §
A.4
, we extend Theorem
1
to a special case where non-primitive wall-crossing occurs.
In §
A.5
, we state a generalisation of Theorem
1
under the weaker assumption
(\star)
that
H^{3}
divides
H^{\prime}.H^{2}
for all
H^{\prime}\in\Pic(\mathfrak{Y})
. Finally, in §
A.6
, we strengthen
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
under
assumption
(\star)
.
The proofs of these results are collected in §
A.7
.

Notation:
In this section, we label the charges by the Chern character (rather than the Mukai vector), so for instance
\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{v})
counts
H
-Gieseker semi-stable sheaves of Chern character
\mathsf{v}
. Furthermore, for readability we decompose
\mathsf{v}
into
its components in
H^{0}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathds{Z})
,
H^{2}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathds{Z})
,
H^{4}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathds{Q})
and
H^{6}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathds{Q})
. For example,

\mathsf{v}=(-1,0,\beta,-m)
stands for
\mathsf{v}=-1+\beta-m
. We also use
the notation
\ch_{\leq 2}E
for the projection of
\ch E
on
H^{0}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathds{Z})\oplus H^{2}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathds{Z})\oplus H^{4}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathds{Q})
.

A.1.
Stable pair invariants from rank 0 DT invariants with minimal D4-brane charge

For any
\beta\in H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
(which can also be regarded as a class in

H^{4}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
by Poincaré duality), we define the integer

(A.1)

\mathcal{C}(\beta)\ \coloneqq\ \left\{\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{cc}\ \ \left\lfloor\frac{2}{3H^{3}}(\beta.H)^{2}+\frac{\beta.H}{3}\right\rfloor&\ \ \ \text{if $\beta.H<H^{3}$,}\\
\ \ \left\lfloor\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta.H)^{2}+\frac{\beta.H}{2}\right\rfloor&\ \ \ \text{if $H^{3}\leq\beta.H$,}\end{array}\right.

which determines the Castelnuovo bound as we explain below.
Consider the function

(A.2)

f(x)\ \coloneqq\ \left\{\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{cl}\vskip 2.84544ptx+\frac{1}{2}&\text{if $0<x<1$,}\\
\vskip 2.84544pt\sqrt{2x+\frac{1}{4}}&\text{if $1<x<\frac{15}{8}$,}\\
\vskip 2.84544pt\frac{2}{3}x+\frac{3}{4}&\text{if $\frac{15}{8}\leq x<\frac{9}{4}$,}\\
\vskip 2.84544pt\frac{1}{3}x+\frac{3}{2}&\text{if $\frac{9}{4}\leq x<3$,}\\
\vskip 2.84544pt\frac{1}{2}x+1&\text{if $3\leq x$.}\end{array}\right.

Theorem 1
.

Fix
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\beta\in H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
\beta.H>0
and

(A.3)

f\left(\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}\right)<-\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}\,.

Then

(A.4)

\begin{split}&{\rm P}_{m,\beta}=\\
&\sum_{(m^{\prime},\,\beta^{\prime})\,\in\,M_{m,\,\beta}}(-1)^{\chi_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}}\chi_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\ {\rm P}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\ \overline{\Omega}_{H}\left(0,\ H,\ \frac{1}{2}H^{2}-\beta^{\prime}+\beta\ ,\ \frac{1}{6}H^{3}+m^{\prime}-m-\beta^{\prime}.H\right),\end{split}

where

(A.5)

\chi_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}=\beta.H+\beta^{\prime}.H+m-m^{\prime}-\frac{H^{3}}{6}-\frac{1}{12}c_{2}(\mathfrak{Y}).H\,.

The set
M_{m,\,\beta}
consists of pairs
(m^{\prime},\,\beta^{\prime})\in H_{0}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
such that

(A.6)

0\leq\beta^{\prime}.H\leq\frac{H^{3}}{2}+\frac{3mH^{3}}{2\beta.H}+\beta.H

and

(A.7)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{C}(\beta^{\prime})\leq\ m^{\prime}\ \leq\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta.H-\beta^{\prime}.H)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\beta.H+\beta^{\prime}.H)+m.

Since
f(x)>\frac{1}{2}
, (
A.3
) implies that
\frac{1}{2}<-\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}
, thus
\beta^{\prime}.H<\beta.H
in (
A.6
).

Remark 1
.

Here are three comments regarding Theorem
1
: a) to prove Theorem
1
, we only need a weaker version of BMT conjecture explained in Remark
3
, b) one can strengthen Theorem
1
for specific CY threefolds (e.g. quintic threefolds) where a stronger version of Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality holds, see Remark
4
for more details, and c) a generalisation of Theorem
1
, when
\mathfrak{Y}
is not of Picard rank one but satisfies assumption
(\star)
, is proved in Proposition
2
.

For any
(m,\beta)\in H_{0}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
, consider the function
\Psi(x,x^{\prime},\alpha)
defined in (
4.24
) for
x=\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}
and
\alpha=-\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}
. We define the function
\Psi_{m,\beta}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as

(A.8)

\displaystyle\Psi_{m,\,\beta}(x^{\prime})\coloneqq\Psi\left(\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}},\ x^{\prime},\ -\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\left(\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}-x^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\left(\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}+x^{\prime}\right)+\frac{m}{H^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\,x^{\prime 2}+\frac{1}{2}\,x^{\prime}\ .

Note that
\Psi_{m,\beta}(x^{\prime})
is the difference of the right and the left hand side of (
A.7
), up to a factor of
H^{3}
, for
x^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}.H/H^{3}
.

Definition 1
.

A value of
(m,\beta)\in H_{0}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
is called optimal if

•

the inequality (
A.3
) is satisfied, and

•

\Psi_{m,\beta}(\frac{1}{H^{3}})<0
and
\Psi_{m,\beta}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}+\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}\right)<0
(i.e.
\Psi_{m,\beta}
is negative for all possible positive values of
\beta^{\prime}.H
in (
A.6
)).

For an optimal value of
(m,\beta)
, the only possible value of
\beta^{\prime}
in the wall-crossing formula (
A.4
) is
\beta^{\prime}=0
. Since
{\rm P}_{m^{\prime},0}=1
if
m^{\prime}=0
and otherwise vanishes, we get the following:

Corollary 1
.

For any optimal value
(m,\beta)\in H_{0}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
,
we have

(A.9)

{\rm P}_{m,\beta}=(-1)^{1+\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(H),\mathsf{w})}\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(H),\mathsf{w})\ \overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{w}),

where
\mathsf{w}=\left(0,H,\beta+\frac{H^{2}}{2},-m+\frac{H^{3}}{6}\right)
.

A.2.
Application I: An explicit formula for minimal rank zero DT invariants

Corollary
1
gives us a way to write arbitrary minimal rank zero DT invariants in terms of PT invariants. Pick
\beta_{0}\in H^{4}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathbb{Q})
and
m_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
After tensoring by the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(kH)
with
k\in\mathds{Z}
,
the rank 0 DT invariant is unchanged,

(A.10)

\overline{\Omega}_{H}(0,H,\beta_{0},m_{0})=\overline{\Omega}_{H}\left(0,H,\beta_{0}+kH^{2},m_{0}+k\beta_{0}.H+\frac{k^{2}}{2}H^{3}\right).

Then as a consequence of Corollary
1
, one gets the following.

Corollary 2
.

There exists
k(\beta_{0},m_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that for any integer
k\geq k(\beta_{0},m_{0})
, the class
(m,\beta)
for

(A.11)

\beta\coloneqq\beta_{0}+kH^{2}-\frac{H^{2}}{2}\ ,\qquad m\coloneqq\frac{H^{3}}{6}-m_{0}-k\beta_{0}.H-\frac{k^{2}}{2}H^{3}

is optimal (see Definition
1
), thus

(A.12)

\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{w})=(-1)^{1+\chi\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}((1-k)H),\,\mathsf{w}\big)}\frac{{\rm P}_{m,\beta}}{\chi\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}((1-k)H),\,\mathsf{w}\big)}\,,

where
\mathsf{w}=\left(0,H,\beta_{0},m_{0}\right)
.

A.3.
Application II: Castelnuovo bound for PT invariants

As a result of wall-crossing for rank
-1
classes and induction on
\beta.H
, we can prove the following Castelnuovo-type bound for stable pairs.

Theorem 2
.

Fix
\beta\in H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
and
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}\xrightarrow{s}F
be a stable pair such that
\ch_{2}(F)=\beta
and
\ch_{3}(F)=m
, then

(A.13)

m\geq-\mathcal{C}(\beta).

In particular, the invariant
{\rm P}_{m,\beta}
vanishes unless the inequality (
A.13
) is satisfied.

The nature of the proof of Theorem
2
is similar to
[
72
, Proposition 1.3]
where the same result is proved under the extra assumption that for any
E\in\Coh(\mathfrak{Y})
, we have
\ch_{2}(E)\in\frac{H^{2}}{2}{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\ch_{3}(E)\in\frac{H^{3}}{6}{\mathbb{Z}}
. Theorem
2
for quintic threefolds, as well as the first part of Theorem
3
below, is also proved in the recent paper
[
73
]
via different arguments.

Remark 2
.

Note that when
m
is close to the Castelnuovo bound
-\mathcal{C}(\beta)
, then one can apply Theorem
1
to find an explicit formula for
{\rm P}_{m,\beta}
in terms of rank zero invariants
\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{w})
with
\ch_{1}(\mathsf{w})=H
. Since
\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{w})=\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{w}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(kH))
for any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the knowledge of a few rank zero DT invariants determines PT invariants along the boundary of Castelnuovo bound.

Let us spell out Remark
2
for the case of the quintic threefold
X_{5}
. Based on physical arguments in §
5.2
, the following vanishings are expected (here
\kappa=H^{3}=5
):

Conjecture 1
.

(i)

\overline{\Omega}_{H}\Bigl(0,\,H,\,\pm(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\kappa})H^{2},\,\frac{H^{3}}{6}\Bigr)=0
,

(ii)

\overline{\Omega}_{H}\Bigl(0,\,H,\,\pm(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{\kappa})H^{2},\,(-\frac{m}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{6})H^{3}\Bigr)=0
for
m=0,-1
.

Theorem 3
.

Take an integer
\mu\geq 13
or
\mu=10
. If
\mu\overset{\kappa}{\equiv}0
and
m=-\,\mathcal{C}(\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2})
, then

(A.14)

{\rm P}_{m,\,\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2}}=(-1)^{m+\mu}(5-m-\mu)\times 5.

If
\mu\overset{\kappa}{\equiv}\pm p
where
p=1\ (\text{resp. }2)
and Conjecture
1
(i) (resp. Conjecture
1
(ii)) holds, then
{\rm P}_{m,\,\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2}}
vanishes if
m<-\,\mathcal{C}(\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2})+p
; moreover, for
m=-\,\mathcal{C}(\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2})+p
, then

(A.15)

{\rm P}_{m,\ \frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2}}=(-1)^{1+\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(H),\mathsf{w})}\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(H),\mathsf{w})\ \overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{w}),

where
\mathsf{w}=\Bigl(0,\,H,\,\big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mu}{\kappa}\big)H^{2},\,\big(-\frac{m}{\kappa}+\frac{1}{6}\big)H^{3}\Bigr)
.

A.4.
An example with non-primitive wall-crossing

In this part, we generalize Theorem
1
for some examples of pairs
(m,\beta)
where the inequality (
A.3
) is saturated.
Similar results can be obtained when the inequality is mildly violated.

Proposition 1
.

If
\beta.H>4H^{3}
and
\alpha=-\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}=f(x)
, where

(x,\alpha)
are defined in (
4.11
),
then Theorem
1
holds true. However if
\beta.H=4H^{3}
and
m=-8H^{3}
, then there is an additional wall with non-primitive wall-crossing, leading to

(A.16)

\begin{split}{\rm P}_{m,\beta}=&\sum_{(m^{\prime},\,\beta^{\prime})\,\in\,M_{m,\,\beta}}(-1)^{\chi_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}}\chi_{m^{\prime},\,\beta^{\prime}}{\rm P}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\ \overline{\Omega}_{H}\left(0,\ H,\ \frac{1}{2}H^{2}-\beta^{\prime}+\beta\ ,\ \frac{1}{6}H^{3}+m^{\prime}-m-\beta^{\prime}.H\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\,\big(\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H))\big)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H))\ ,\end{split}

where we recall that
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H))=\chi_{2\mathcal{D}}=\frac{4}{3}\kappa+\frac{1}{6}c_{2}
.

A.5.
Relaxing the Picard rank one assumption

In this subsection, we relax the Picard rank one assumption to

Assumption
(\star)
:

H^{3}
divides
H^{\prime}.H^{2}
for all
H^{\prime}\in\Pic(\mathfrak{Y})
.

The following proposition generalises Theorem
1
.

Proposition 2
.

Let
(\mathfrak{Y},H)
be a smooth polarised Calabi-Yau threefold
\mathfrak{Y}
satisfying the BMT conjecture and assumption
(\star)
.
Fix
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\beta\in H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
such that the condition (
A.3
) is satisfied. Then

(A.17)

\begin{split}&{\rm P}_{m,\beta}=\\
&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{l}(H^{\prime},\,m^{\prime},\,\beta^{\prime})\,\in\,\widetilde{M}_{m,\,\beta}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\chi_{H^{\prime},m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}}\chi_{H^{\prime},m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\ {\rm P}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\ \overline{\Omega}_{H}\left(0,\ H^{\prime},\ \frac{1}{2}H^{\prime 2}-\beta^{\prime}+\beta\ ,\ \frac{1}{6}H^{\prime 3}+m^{\prime}-m-\beta^{\prime}.H^{\prime}\right),\end{split}

where

(A.18)

\chi_{H^{\prime},m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}=\beta.H^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}.H^{\prime}+m-m^{\prime}-\frac{H^{\prime 3}}{6}-\frac{1}{12}c_{2}(\mathfrak{Y}).H^{\prime}\,,

and the set
\widetilde{M}_{m,\,\beta}
consists of triples
(H^{\prime},m^{\prime},\,\beta^{\prime})\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Q})\oplus H_{0}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus H_{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})
such that

(i)

H^{\prime}.H^{2}=H^{3}
,

(ii)

0\leq\beta^{\prime}.H\leq\frac{H^{\prime 2}.H}{2}+\frac{3mH^{3}}{2\beta.H}+\beta.H
,

(iii)

m^{\prime}\ \geq\ -\frac{2}{3}\beta^{\prime}.H\left(\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\right)
, and

(iv)

m^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2H^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}H^{\prime 2}H-\beta^{\prime}.H+\beta.H\right)^{2}+\frac{H^{3}}{24}-\frac{H^{\prime 3}}{6}+m+\beta^{\prime}.H^{\prime}
.

A.6.
Rank 0 DT invariants from rank one DT and PT invariants

Finally, we provide a strengthening of the wall-crossing formula
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
for rank zero classes, which holds for CY threefolds satisfying the BMT inequality and assumption
(\star)
.

Given a polarization
H\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathds{Z})
and a rank 0 Chern class
21
21

21

Note that
the sign of
m
is flipped compared to
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
.

(A.19)

\mathsf{v}=[0,D,\beta,-m]\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{4}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{6}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathbb{Q})\,,

with
D\neq 0
,
let us define

(A.20)

Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{D.H^{2}}{H^{3}}\right)^{2}+6\left(\frac{\beta.H}{D.H^{2}}\right)^{2}+\frac{12m}{D.H^{2}}\,.

Theorem 4
.

Let
(\mathfrak{Y},H)
be a smooth polarised CY threefold
\mathfrak{Y}
satisfying the BMT conjecture and assumption
(\star)
. If a rank zero class
\mathsf{v}=[0,D,\beta,-m]
satisfies
Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})<\min\{\frac{r^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{8},\,r-\frac{1}{2}\}
for
r=\frac{D.H^{2}}{H^{3}}
then

(A.21)

\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{v})=\big(\#H^{2}(X,{\mathbb{Z}})_{\mathrm{tors}}\big)^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{v}_{1}\,=\,-e^{D_{1}}(1,0,-\beta_{1},-m_{1})\\
\mathsf{v}_{2}\,=\,e^{D_{2}}(1,0,-\beta_{2},-m_{2})\\
\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2}\,=\,\mathsf{v}\\
(D_{i},\ \beta_{i},\ m_{i})\,\in\,M_{i}\left(\mathsf{v}\right)\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\chi(\mathsf{v}_{2},\mathsf{v}_{1})-1}\;\chi(\mathsf{v}_{2},\mathsf{v}_{1})\,{\mathrm{P}}_{-m_{1},\beta_{1}}\ {\mathrm{I}}_{m_{2},\beta_{2}}\,.

Here
M_{i}(\mathsf{v})
for
i=1,2
is the set all classes
(D_{i},\,\beta_{i},\,m_{i})\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{4}(X,\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{6}(X,\mathbb{Q})
such that

(A.22)

\left|\frac{D_{i}.H}{H^{3}}-\theta_{i}\right|<1\qquad\text{for}\qquad\theta_{i}\coloneqq\frac{\beta.H}{D.H^{2}}+(-1)^{i}\sqrt{\frac{r^{2}}{4}-\frac{1}{2}Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})}\ ,

(A.23)

-\frac{D_{i}^{2}.H}{2H^{3}}+\frac{\beta_{i}.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{\beta.H}{D.H^{2}}\frac{D_{i}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}\leq-\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{D.H^{2}}{H^{3}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\beta.H}{D.H^{2}}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}Q_{H}(\mathsf{v}),

and

(A.24)

(-1)^{i+1}m_{i}\leq\ \frac{2}{3}\beta_{i}.H\left(\frac{\beta_{i}.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\right).

Note that if
Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})
is as small as required in
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
, then one can apply
[
8
, Proposition 3.5 & 3.6]
to show that the above sets
M_{i}(\mathsf{v})
reduce to
M(\mathsf{v})
so that the final wall-crossing formulae agree.

A.7.
Proofs

In this subsection, we collect the proofs of the various claims up to now.

To prove Theorem
1
, we use weak stability conditions
\nu_{b,w}
for
(b,w)\in U
where
U=\{(b,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\colon w>\frac{b^{2}}{2}\}
, see Fig.
2
and
[
6
, §1]
for all details. We study wall-crossing for the fixed class

(A.25)

\mathsf{v}=(-1,0,\beta,-m)\in\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i=0\,}^{3}}H^{2i}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathbb{Q}).

We start in the large volume limit
b>0
and
w\gg 1
where an object
E\in\mathcal{A}_{b}
of class
\mathsf{v}
is
\nu_{b,w}
-semistable if and only if
E^{\vee}\otimes\,{\rm det}\,(E)^{-1}[1]
is a stable pair, see

[
4
, §3]
. Then we move down and investigate all walls of instability for objects of class
\mathsf{v}
.

By the conjectural BMT inequality (
2.38
), if there is a
\nu_{b,w}
-semistable object in
\mathcal{A}_{b}
of class
\mathsf{v}
, then
L_{b,w}(\mathsf{v})=w(2H^{3}\beta.H)+3b(-H^{3})(-m)+2(\beta.H)^{2}\geq 0
, i.e.

(A.26)

w\geq-b\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}-\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}\,.

Hence any wall for class
\mathsf{v}
lies above or on the line
\ell_{f}
of equation
w=\alpha b-x
where

(A.27)

\alpha=-\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}\qquad\text{and}\qquad x=\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}\,.

Let
b_{1}<b_{2}
be the values of
b
at the intersection points of the line
\ell_{f}
with the boundary
\partial U
.

Assume there is a wall
\ell
for class
\mathsf{v}
. Then there is an object
E\in\mathcal{A}_{b}
of class
\mathsf{v}
which is strictly
\nu_{b,w}
-semistable for all
(b,w)\in\ell\cap U
. Let
E^{\prime}\rightarrow E\rightarrow E^{\prime\prime}
be a destabilising sequence along the wall
\ell
.

Lemma 1
.

Suppose

(i)

0<b_{1}<2
,

(ii)

1<b_{2}-b_{1}
, and

(iii)

if
b_{2}r\leq c\leq b_{1}(r+1)
for some
(r,c)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, then either
(r,c)=(0,1)
or
(0,0)
.

Then
b_{1}\geq 1
and there is an ordering
E_{0},E_{1}
of
E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}
such that

•

E_{0}
is a rank zero Gieseker-stable sheaf with
\ch_{1}(E_{0})=H
.

•

E_{1}
is a rank
-1
object and
\nu_{b,w}
-stable for
b>-1
and
w\gg 1
.

Moreover, there is no
\nu_{b,w}
-semistable object of class
\mathsf{v}
for
(b,w)\in U
below
\ell_{f}
.

Proof.

Since
\rk(E)=-1
, one of the objects
E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}
has rank
\,<0
; call it
E_{1}
. The other
E_{0}
has rank
\,\geq 0
. Let

(A.28)

\ch_{\leq 1}(E_{0})=(r,cH)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ch_{\leq 1}(E_{1})=(-1-r,-cH).

By
[
6
, Remark 1.2]
for any point
(b,w)\in\ell\cap U
, we have
\ch_{1}(E_{i})H^{2}-b\ch_{0}(E_{i})H^{3}\geq 0
for
i=1,2
. Since
\ell
lies above
\ell_{f}
, this in particular holds for
b=b_{1},b_{2}
which implies

(A.29)

b_{2}r\leq c\leq b_{1}(1+r).

By assumption (iii), it follows that either
(r,c)=(0,0)
or
(r,c)=(0,1)
. In the first case,
E_{0}
is of
\nu_{b,w}
-slope
+\infty
for all
(b,w)\in U
, thus it cannot have the same
\nu_{b,w}
-slope as
E
along the wall
\ell
. Thus
(r,c)=(0,1)
and
1\leq b_{1}
.

Hence
E_{1}
is a rank
-1
object with
\ch_{1}(E_{1})=-H
. By
[
108
, Lemma 3.5]
, there is no wall for
E_{1}
crossing the vertical line
b=2
, so if
E_{1}
is
\nu_{b,w}
-semistable for some
b=2
and
w>2
, then it is
\nu_{b,w}
-stable for
b=2
and any
w>2
. By conditions (i) and (ii), the wall
\ell_{f}
and so the wall
\ell
intersects the vertical line
b=2
at a point inside
U
, thus
\nu_{b,w}
-semistability of
E_{1}
along the wall implies that
E_{1}
is
\nu_{b,w}
-stable for
b=2
and
w\gg 1
. Then the wall and chamber structure for the class
\ch(E_{1})
implies that
E_{1}
is
\nu_{b,w}
-stable for any
b>-1
and
w\gg 1
.

We claim that there is no wall for
E_{0}
when we move up from the wall
\ell
to the large volume limit. Suppose for a contradiction that there was a wall
\ell_{0}
with the destabilising sequence
F_{1}\hookrightarrow E_{0}\twoheadrightarrow F_{2}
. Set
r_{i}:=\rk\!\left(\mathcal{H}^{-1}(F_{i})\right)\geq 0
. By definition of the heart
\mathcal{A}_{b}
, for
(b,w)\in\ell_{0}\cap U
,

(A.30)

\ch_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}^{-1}(F_{i})\right).H^{2}\leq b\,r_{i}H^{3}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ch_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}^{0}(F_{i})\right).H^{2}\geq b\,(\ch_{0}(F_{i})+r_{i})H^{3}.

Since
\ell_{0}
lies above or on
\ell_{f}
, we may apply the left hand inequality for
b=b_{1}
and the right hand for
b=b_{2}
. Then subtracting gives

\ch_{1}(F_{i}).H^{2}\ \geq\ b_{2}\ch_{0}(F_{i})H^{3}+(b_{2}-b_{1})r_{i}H^{3}
.
Adding over
i=0,1
gives

(A.31)

H^{3}=\ch_{1}(E).H^{2}\ \geq\ (b_{2}-b_{1})(r_{0}+r_{1})H^{3}.

Since
b_{2}-b_{1}>1
by assumption (ii), we get
r_{1}=r_{2}=0
. Thus
\mathcal{H}^{-1}(F_{i})=0
as they are torsion-free sheaves by definition of the heart
\mathcal{A}_{b}
. Thus
F_{i}
’s are sheaves with
\ch_{0}(F_{1})=\ch_{0}(F_{2})=0
. Hence they have the same
\nu_{b,w}
-slope as
E_{0}
with respect to any
(b,w)\in U
, so they cannot induce a wall. Thus
E_{0}
is
\nu_{b,w}
-semistable for
w\gg 1
, hence is a torsion sheaf by
[
10
, Lemma 2.7(c)]
.

By definition of the heart
\mathcal{A}_{b}
, any torsion sheaf
F
lies in
\mathcal{A}_{b}
and
\nu_{b,w}(F)=\frac{\ch_{2}(F).H}{\ch_{1}(F).H^{2}}
if
\ch_{1}(F)\neq 0
, otherwise
\nu_{b,w}(F)=+\infty
. In our case, since
\ch_{1}(E_{0}).H^{2}=H^{3}
is minimal, the
\nu_{b,w\gg 1}
-semistability of
E_{0}
immediately implies that
E_{0}
is a Gieseker-stable sheaf.

∎

Recall that the equation of
\ell_{f}
is
w=\alpha b-x
for the values of
\alpha,x
in (
A.27
). The
b
-values of the intersection point of
\ell_{f}
with
\partial U
, which is the parabola of equation
w=\frac{1}{2}b^{2}
, are

(A.32)

b_{1}=\alpha-\sqrt{\alpha^{2}-2x},\quad b_{2}=\alpha+\sqrt{\alpha^{2}-2x}\,.

The condition (i) that
0<b_{1}<2
is equivalent to

(A.33)

\alpha-\sqrt{\alpha^{2}-2x}<2\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad 0<\alpha<2\qquad\text{or}\qquad 1+\frac{x}{2}<\alpha\,.

Also the condition (ii) that
b_{2}-b_{1}>1
is equivalent to

(A.34)

2\sqrt{\alpha^{2}-2x}>1\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad\sqrt{2x+\frac{1}{4}}<\alpha\,.

Hence, a simple case by case analysis verifies the following:

Lemma 2
.

Consider the function
f(x)
defined in (
A.2
). If
x>1
and
\alpha>f(x)
, then the conditions (i), (ii) and (iii) in Lemma
1
hold.

By Lemma
1
, the destabilising objects are of Chern character

(A.35)

\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\coloneqq\ch(E_{0})=\left(0,\ H,\ \frac{1}{2}H^{2}-\beta^{\prime}+\beta,\ \frac{1}{6}H^{3}+m^{\prime}-m-\beta^{\prime}.H\right)

and

(A.36)

\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\coloneqq\ch(E_{1})=\left(-1,\ -H,\ -\frac{H^{2}}{2}+\beta^{\prime},\ -\frac{1}{6}H^{3}-m^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}.H\right).

We know that the point
\varpi(\ch(E_{1}))=(1\,,\,-\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{1}{2})
(defined in (
2.36
)) lies above or on
\ell_{f}
, so

(A.37)

\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{H^{3}}\,\leq\,\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}+\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\,.

Moreover, applying
[
6
, Lemma B.3]
for
E_{0}
implies

(A.38)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}H^{3}+m^{\prime}-m-\beta^{\prime}.H\leq\frac{1}{2H^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}H^{3}-\beta^{\prime}.H+\beta.H\right)^{2}+\frac{H^{3}}{24}

which is equivalent to

(A.39)

m^{\prime}\ \leq\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta.H-\beta^{\prime}.H)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\beta.H+\beta^{\prime}.H)+m\,.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Suppose
{\rm P}_{m,\beta}\neq 0
, i.e. there is a
\nu_{b,w}
-stable object of class
\mathsf{v}=(-1,0,\beta,-m)
for
b>0
and
w\gg 1
. By
[
108
, Lemma 3.5]
, there is no wall for class
\mathsf{v}
crossing the vertical line
b=1
. Then the conjectural BMT inequality (
2.38
) at the boundary point
(b,w)=(1,\frac{1}{2})
implies that

(A.40)

-\frac{m}{H^{3}}\ \leq\ \frac{2}{3}\left(\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}\right)^{2}+\frac{\beta.H}{3H^{3}}

which proves the claim (
A.13
) if
\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}\leq 1
. Hence we may assume
\beta.H^{3}>H^{3}
. If (
A.13
) does not hold, then

(A.41)

-\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}>\frac{3\beta.H}{4H^{3}}+\frac{3}{4}>f\left(\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}\right).

Then by combining Lemma
1
and Lemma
2
, it follows that as we move down from the large volume limit, any large volume limit stable object
E
of class
\mathsf{v}
gets destabilised along a wall with the destabilising objects
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
as described in Lemma
1
. By
[
10
, Corollary 3.10]
, we know that the discriminants
\Delta_{H}
of the destabilising factors are less than
\Delta_{H}(\mathsf{v})
, so
\beta^{\prime}.H<\beta.H
. Thus by applying induction on
\beta.H
, we may assume the object
E_{1}
, which is large volume limit stable of rank
-1
, satisfies the claim. Combining it with (
A.39
) implies that

(A.42)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta^{\prime}.H)^{2}-\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{2}\leq\ m^{\prime}\ \leq\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta.H-\beta^{\prime}.H)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\beta.H+\beta^{\prime}.H)+m.

This in particular implies that

0\leq\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta.H-\beta^{\prime}.H)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\beta.H+\beta^{\prime}.H)+\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta^{\prime}.H)^{2}+\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{2}+m.

If the claim (
A.13
) does not hold, then

\displaystyle 0<\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta.H-\beta^{\prime}.H)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\beta.H+\beta^{\prime}.H)+\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta^{\prime}.H)^{2}+\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{2}+\left(-\frac{1}{2H^{3}}(\beta.H)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta.H\right)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}.H\left(\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{H^{3}}-\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}+1\right).

Since
\beta^{\prime}.H\geq 0
, we get
\beta.H-H^{3}<\beta^{\prime}.H
, then (
A.37
) gives
\beta.H-H^{3}<\frac{3mH^{3}}{2\beta.H}+\beta.H+\frac{H^{3}}{2}
, i.e.
-\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}<\frac{3}{2}
which is not possible by (
A.41
) for
\beta.H>H^{3}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1
.

By
[
4
, §3]
, any
\nu_{b,w}
-semistable object
E\in\mathcal{A}_{b}
of class
\mathsf{v}
for
b>0
and
w\gg 1
is derived-dual of a stable pair up to tensoring by a line bundle with torsion
c_{1}
, so

(A.43)

\overline{\Omega}_{\infty}(\mathsf{v})\coloneqq\overline{\Omega}_{b\,>0,\ w\,\rightarrow\,+\infty}(\mathsf{v})=\big(\#H^{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})_{\mathrm{tors}}\big){\rm P}_{m,\beta}.

On the other hand, we know that there is no
\nu_{b,w}
-semistable object of class
\mathsf{v}
when
(b,w)\in U
lies below
\ell_{f}
, so
\overline{\Omega}_{b,w}(\mathsf{v})=0
. Between, this point and large volume limit, there are several walls
\ell
.

If
0<x\leq 1
and
\alpha>f(x)
, then
b_{1}<1
, implying
\operatorname{PT}_{m,\beta}=0
and no non-trivial wall exists, so the claim follows. Therefore, we may assume
x>1
. Subsequently, by applying Lemma
2
, we can utilize Lemma
1
, which describes the destabilizing factors along any wall
\ell
. We know that the first factor
E_{0}
is Gieseker-stable, and any Gieseker-stable sheaf of class
\ch(E_{0})
is
\nu_{b,w}
-stable for
(b,w)\in\ell
as there is no wall for
\ch(E_{0})
between the large volume limit and
\ell
. Thus for points
(b,w^{\pm})
above and below the wall
\ell
, we have

(A.44)

\overline{\Omega}_{b,w^{\pm}}(\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})=\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}).

We also know that there is no wall for the other factor
E_{1}
between
\ell
and the large volume limit, thus

(A.45)

\overline{\Omega}_{b,w^{\pm}}(\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})=\overline{\Omega}_{\,b>1,\ w\,\rightarrow\,+\infty}(\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})=\big(\#H^{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})_{\mathrm{tors}}\big){\rm P}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}.

Combining (
A.37
), (
A.39
) and Theorem
2
implies that
(\beta^{\prime},m^{\prime})\in M_{m,\beta}
as defined in Theorem
1
.

Then summing up the wall crossing formulae
[
3
, Equation (5.13)]
over all walls for class
\mathsf{v}
between the large volume limit and
\ell_{f}
gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\ =\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,\,w\,<\,\alpha b-x}(\mathsf{v})

(A.46)

\displaystyle\ =\overline{\Omega}_{\infty}(\mathsf{v})\ +

\displaystyle\ \sum_{(m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\,\in\,M_{m,\beta}}(-1)^{\chi(\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}},\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})+1}\chi(\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}},\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})\big(\#H^{2}(\mathfrak{Y},{\mathbb{Z}})_{\mathrm{tors}}\big)\ {\rm P}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\ \overline{\Omega}_{H}\left(\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\right).

This implies

(A.47)

\displaystyle{\rm P}_{m,\beta}=\sum_{(m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\,\in\,M_{m,\beta}}(-1)^{\chi(\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}},\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})}\chi(\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}},\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})\ {\rm P}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\ \overline{\Omega}_{H}\left(\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\right),

where
\chi(\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}},\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}})=\beta.H+\beta^{\prime}.H+m-m^{\prime}-\frac{H^{3}}{6}-\frac{1}{12}c_{2}(\mathfrak{Y}).H=\chi_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
as claimed.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Suppose
\mu=n\kappa+p
where
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
p=0,\pm 1,\pm 2
. One can easily check that if
\mu\geq 13
or
\mu=10
, the classes (i)
\left(\mathcal{C}(\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2}),\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2}\right)
and (ii)
\left(\mathcal{C}(\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2})-1,\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2}\right)
for
p=\pm 1,\pm 2
, and if
\mu\geq 18
the class (iii)
\left(\mathcal{C}(\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2})-2,\frac{\mu}{\kappa}H^{2}\right)
for
p=\pm 2
are optimal in the sense of Definition
1
. Thus combining Corollary
1
and Conjecture
1
implies the claim. Note that when
\mu=n\kappa
,
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
implies that

(A.48)

\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}_{H}\left(0,H,\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)H^{2},\left(\frac{1}{6}+\frac{n(n+1)}{2}\right)H^{3}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,(-1)^{\chi(\mathcal{O}((n+1)H),\mathcal{O}(nH))+1}\chi(\mathcal{O}((n+1)H),\mathcal{O}(nH)[1])=5.

If
\mu=13
or
17
, one can directly apply the wall-crossing formula
A.4
in Theorem
1
to show again
\beta^{\prime}=0
which implies the claim.
∎

Remark 3
.

To prove Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
(and so Corollary
1
and
2
, and Theorem
3
), we applied the conjectural BMT inequality only for (i) the rank zero classes
\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
to get (
A.56
) using
[
6
, Lemma B.3]
, and (ii) rank
-1
class
(-1,0,\beta,-m)
with
\beta.H\leq H^{3}
to get (
A.40
). Thus we only need the conjectural BMT inequality for the following two cases:

(i)

Rank zero Gieseker-stable sheaves of class
(0,H,\beta_{1},m_{1})
for the values
(b,w)\in U
lying along the line
\ell_{1}
which is of slope
\frac{\beta_{1}.H}{H^{3}}
and intersects
\partial U
at two points with
b
-values
b^{\prime}<b^{\prime\prime}
so that
b^{\prime\prime}-b^{\prime}=1
.

(ii)

Rank one torsion-free sheaves of class
(1,0,-\beta_{2},m_{2})
for
\beta_{2}.H\leq H^{3}
and the point
(b,w)=(-1,\frac{1}{2})
along the boundary
\partial U
.

This, in particular, shows that the weaker version of BMT conjecture proved in
[
44
]
for quintic
X_{5}
and in
[
45
]
for
X_{4,2}
is sufficient for our result.

Remark 4
.

Suppose our CY threefold
\mathfrak{Y}
satisfies a stronger version of classical Bogolomov-Gieseker inequality (
2.27
), i.e. there is a function
G\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that any slope-semistable sheaf
E
satisfies
\frac{\ch_{2}(E).H}{\ch_{0}(E)H^{3}}\leq G\left(\frac{\ch_{1}(E).H^{2}}{\ch_{0}(E)H^{3}}\right)
and
G(b)\leq\frac{b^{2}}{2}
for all
b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then one can enlarge the space of weak stability conditions
U
to
U_{G}\coloneqq\{(b,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\colon w>G(b)\}
, and apply all the arguments in this section within the enlarged space
U_{G}
instead of
U
. This, in particular, shows that the intersection of the line
\ell_{f}
with
\partial U_{G}
has
b
-values
b_{1}^{G}<b_{2}^{G}
so that
b_{2}^{G}-b_{1}^{G}\geq b_{2}-b_{1}
, thus we can improve the function
f
in Theorem
1
. For instance, for a quintic threefold, one can apply Li’s version of stronger Bogolomov-Gieseker inequality
[
44
, Theorem 1.1]
to show that equation of the function
f
can be improved to
\frac{x}{2}+1
for any
x>0
(see Figures
3
and
6
).

Proof of Proposition
1
.

Since
\alpha=\frac{x}{2}+1
, the line
\ell_{f}
intersects
\partial U
at two points with
b
-values
b_{1}=2<b_{2}
such that
b_{2}-b_{1}\geq b_{1}
, where the inequality is strict if
x>4
. Then, using the same notations as in Lemma
1
, it implies that
b_{2}r\leq c\leq 2(r+1)
. Thus if
x>4
, we can have (i)
(r,c)=(0,1)
, or (ii)
(r,c)=(0,2)
. If
x=4
, there is a third possibility (iii)
r=1
and
c=4
.

First consider a wall
\ell
of type (i). We know
\ell
lies above or on
\ell_{f}
, so there is
w^{+}>2
such that the point
(2,w^{+})\in U
lies just above the wall
\ell
. Since no wall for
E_{1}
can cross the vertical line
b=2
, we get
\overline{\Omega}_{b=2,w^{+}}(\ch(E_{1}))=\overline{\Omega}_{\,b=2,\ w\,\rightarrow\,+\infty}(\ch(E_{1}))
. Thus the proof of Theorem
1
goes through.

In case (ii), we know that
\ch_{\leq 1}(E_{1})=(-1,-2H)
. Thus
\varpi(E_{1})
lies on
\partial U
, so
\ch_{2}(E_{1})=-2H^{2}
. Then
[
109
, Proposition 4.20(ii)]
implies that
E_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H)[1]
, so
\frac{\ch_{2}(E_{0}).H^{2}}{H^{3}}=x+2
and
\ch_{3}(E_{0})=\frac{2}{3}\alpha xH^{3}+\frac{4}{3}H^{3}=\frac{H^{3}}{3}x^{2}+\frac{2H^{3}}{3}x+\frac{4}{3}H^{3}
. Applying
[
6
, Lemma B.3]
for
E_{3}
gives

\frac{\ch_{3}(E_{0})}{H^{3}}=\frac{1}{3}x^{2}+\frac{2}{3}x+\frac{4}{3}\leq\frac{(x+2)^{2}}{4}+\frac{1}{3}

which holds only if
x=4
. If
\beta=4H^{2}
and
m=-8H^{3}
, then
\ch(E_{0})=(0,2H,6H^{2},\frac{28}{3}H^{3})
. Applying the conjectural BMT inequality (
2.38
) implies that the final wall for
E_{0}
coincides with the line
\ell_{f}
and this is the only wall that can happen for
E_{0}
by
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
where the destabilising factors are
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(4H)
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H)[1]
.

In case (iii) when
x=4
, we know that
\varpi(E_{0})
lies along the wall, so
E_{1}
is
of class
\ch_{\leq 2}(E_{0})=(1,4H,8H^{2})
. Since
\Delta_{H}(E_{0})=0
, there is no wall for
E_{0}
up to the large volume limit by
[
10
, Corollary 3.10]
, so
E_{0}
is a slope-stable sheaf and
\ch_{3}(E_{0})\leq\frac{32}{3}H^{3}
. The other factor is of class
\ch_{\leq 2}(E_{1})=(-2,-4H,-4H^{2})
. Applying the BMT inequality (
2.38
) at the point
(b=2+\epsilon,w)
on the wall, where
0<\epsilon\ll 1
, implies that
\ch_{3}(E_{1})\leq-\frac{8}{3}H^{3}
. Given that
\ch_{3}(E_{0})+\ch_{3}(E_{1})=8H^{3}
, it follows that
\ch(E_{0})=\ch(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(4H))
and
\ch(E_{1})=-\ch(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H)^{\oplus 2})
.

To summarise, case (i) only contributes to the walls which are of the same form as described in Lemma
1
. If
x>4
, this is the only case that we need to consider and so Theorem
1
is valid. But if
\beta=4H^{2}
, cases (ii) and (iii) contribute to the last wall
\ell_{f}
. Define
\mathsf{v}_{1}\coloneqq\ch(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H)[1])
and
\mathsf{v}_{2}\coloneqq\ch(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(4H))
, and let
(b,w^{{}^{\pm}})
be points in
U
just above and below the final wall
\ell_{f}
. Then applying the wall-crossing formula
[
8
, Equation (16)]
shows that we have three contributions along this wall:

(1)

\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2}\}
contribution (corresponding to case (ii)) to
\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{-}}(\mathsf{v})
is

(A.49)

(-1)^{\chi(\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2})+1}\chi(\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2})\,\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{+}}(\mathsf{v}_{1})\,\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{+}}(\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2}).

As explained in case (ii), we know that
\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{+}}(\mathsf{v}_{i})=\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{v}_{i})=1
for
i=1,2
and

\begin{split}\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{+}}(\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2})=&(-1)^{\chi(\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2})}\chi(\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2})\,\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{+}}(\mathsf{v}_{1})\,\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{+}}(\mathsf{v}_{2})\end{split}

thus the contribution (
A.49
) is
-\big(\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H))\big)^{2}
.

(2)

\{2\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2}\}
contribution (corresponding to case (iii)) to
\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{-}}(\mathsf{v})
is

2\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H),\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(4H))\times\overline{\Omega}_{H}(2\mathsf{v}_{1})\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{v}_{2}),

where
\overline{\Omega}_{H}(2\mathsf{v}_{1})=\frac{1}{4}\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{v}_{1})=\frac{1}{4}
by
[
3
, Example 6.2]
;

(3)

\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2}\}
contribution to
\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{-}}(\mathsf{v})
is

(A.50)

\frac{1}{2}\big(\chi(\mathsf{v}_{1},\mathsf{v}_{2})\big)^{2}\,\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{v}_{1})^{2}\,\overline{\Omega}_{H}(\mathsf{v}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\big(\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H))\big)^{2}.

Hence the overall contribution of the wall
\ell_{f}
to
\overline{\Omega}_{\,b,w^{-}}(\mathsf{v})
is

(A.51)

-\frac{1}{2}\big(\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H))\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(2H)).

Combining this with the wall-crossing formula (
A.7
) implies the claim.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2
.

As before, we do wall-crossing for the class
\mathsf{v}=(-1,0,\beta,-m)
. The same argument as in Lemma
1
implies that the destabilising factors have Chern class

(A.52)

\mathsf{v}^{0}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},H^{\prime}}=\ch(E_{0})=\left(0,\ H^{\prime},\ \frac{1}{2}H^{\prime 2}-\beta^{\prime}+\beta,\ \frac{1}{6}H^{\prime 3}+m^{\prime}-m-\beta^{\prime}.H^{\prime}\right)

and
\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},H^{\prime}}
where

(A.53)

\mathsf{v}^{1}_{m^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},H^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Y}}(-H^{\prime})=\ch(E_{1}(-H^{\prime}))=\left(-1,\ 0,\ \beta^{\prime},\ -m^{\prime}\right).

where
\frac{H^{\prime}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}=1
. Moreover
E_{0}
is a Gieseker-stable sheaf as
\ch_{1}(E_{0}).H^{2}
is still minimal and
E_{1}(-H^{\prime})
is
\nu_{b,w}
-stable for
b>0
and
w\gg 1
, thus
\beta^{\prime}.H\geq 0
. We know that the point
\varpi(\ch(E_{1}))=(1\,,\,-\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{H.H^{\prime 2}}{2H^{3}})
(defined in (
2.36
)) lies above or on
\ell_{f}
, so

(A.54)

\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{H^{3}}\,\leq\,\frac{3m}{2\beta.H}+\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{H.H^{\prime 2}}{2H^{3}}\,.

Moreover, we know that
E_{1}
is
\nu_{b,w}
-stable for
b>-1
and
w\gg 1
, so
(E_{1}\otimes\,{\rm det}\,(E_{1}))^{\vee}[1]
is a stable pair, thus
[
5
, Proposition 2.6]
implies that

(A.55)

-m^{\prime}\ \leq\ \frac{2}{3}\beta^{\prime}.H\left(\frac{\beta^{\prime}.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\right).

On the other hand, applying
[
6
, Lemma B.3]
for
E_{0}
gives

(A.56)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}H^{\prime 3}+m^{\prime}-m-\beta^{\prime}.H^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2H^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{2}H^{\prime 2}H-\beta^{\prime}.H+\beta.H\right)^{2}+\frac{H^{3}}{24}.

Then the claim follows by a similar argument as in the wall-crossing formula (
A.7
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
4
.

The argument is similar to
[
8
]
, we include it for completeness. Define
r=\frac{D.H^{2}}{H^{3}}
and
s=\frac{\beta.H}{H^{3}}
, then by our assumption
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. The conjectural BMT inequality implies that any wall
\ell
for class
\mathsf{v}
lies above or on the line
\ell_{f}
with equation

(A.57)

w=\frac{s}{r}b+\frac{r^{2}}{8}-\frac{s^{2}}{2r^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})

which intersects
\partial U
at two points with
b
-values

(A.58)

b_{1}=\frac{s}{r}-\sqrt{\frac{r^{2}}{4}-\frac{1}{2}Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})},\quad b_{2}=\frac{s}{r}+\sqrt{\frac{r^{2}}{4}-\frac{1}{2}Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})}\,.

Our assumption on
Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})
implies
b_{2}-b_{1}>\max\{r-1,\ \frac{1}{2}\}
. Let
E_{1}\rightarrow E\rightarrow E_{2}
be a destabilising sequence along a wall
\ell
for class
v
. By definition of the heart
\mathcal{A}_{b}
,

(A.59)

\mu_{H}^{+}(\mathcal{H}^{-1}(E_{i}))\leq b_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad b_{2}\leq\mu_{H}^{-}(\mathcal{H}^{0}(E_{i})).

Summing up over
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
and using
\rk(E)=\rk(E_{1})+\rk(E_{2})=0
imply

(A.60)

r\geq(b_{2}-b_{1})\big(\ch_{0}(\mathcal{H}^{0}(E_{1}))+\ch_{0}(\mathcal{H}^{0}(E_{2}))\big).

Since
b_{2}-b_{1}>\frac{r}{2}
, we get

(A.61)

\ch_{0}(\mathcal{H}^{-1}(E_{1}))+\ch_{0}(\mathcal{H}^{-1}(E_{2}))=\ch_{0}(\mathcal{H}^{0}(E_{1}))+\ch_{0}(\mathcal{H}^{0}(E_{2}))\leq 1.

Therefore, one of the factors
E_{1}
is of rank
-1
with
\mathcal{H}^{-1}(E_{1})
of rank one and
\mathcal{H}^{0}(E_{1})
of rank zero; and the other factor
E_{2}
is a sheaf of rank one. We claim

(A.62)

\mu_{H}(E_{2})-b_{2}<1.

Otherwise, (
A.59
) gives

(A.63)

b_{2}-b_{1}\leq\mu_{H}(E_{2})-1-\mu_{H}(\mathcal{H}^{-1}(E_{1})).

Since
\mathcal{H}^{0}(E_{1})
is of rank zero, we have
\mu_{H}(E_{1})\leq\mu_{H}(\mathcal{H}^{-1}(E_{1}))
, thus

(A.64)

b_{2}-b_{1}\leq\mu_{H}(E_{2})-1-\mu_{H}(E_{1})=r-1.

The last equality comes from
\rk(E_{2})=-\rk(E_{1})=1
. But the above is not possible by our assumption on
b_{2}-b_{1}
. Combining it with (
A.59
) gives

(A.65)

b_{2}\leq\mu_{H}(E_{2})=\frac{\ch_{1}(E_{2}).H^{2}}{H^{3}}<b_{2}+1.

We know that there is no wall for
E_{2}
crossing the vertical lines
b=\mu(E)-\frac{1}{2}
and
b=\mu(E)-1

[
108
, Lemma 3.5]
. Since
b_{2}-b_{1}>\frac{1}{2}
at least one of these vertical lines intersects the wall
\ell
at a point inside
U
. Thus
\nu_{b,w}
-semistability of
E_{2}
along the wall implies that
E_{2}
is
\nu_{b,w}
-stable for
b<\mu(E_{2})
and
w\gg 1
, so
E_{2}
is a rank one torsion-free sheaf. A similar argument also shows that
E_{1}
is stable in the large volume limit, so is the derived dual of a stable pair (up to tensoring by a line bundle).

Hence the destabilising factors are of classes
\mathsf{v}_{i}=(-1)^{i}e^{D_{i}}(1,0,-\beta_{i},-m_{i})
for
i=1,2
. We know that the point
\varpi(E_{i})=\left(\frac{D_{i}.H^{2}}{H^{3}},\ \frac{D_{i}^{2}.H}{2H^{3}}-\frac{\beta_{i}.H}{H^{3}}\right)
lies above or on
\ell_{f}
, i.e.

(A.66)

\frac{D_{i}^{2}.H}{2H^{3}}-\frac{\beta_{i}.H}{H^{3}}-\frac{\beta.H}{D.H^{2}}\frac{D_{i}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}\geq\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{D.H^{2}}{H^{3}}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\beta.H}{D.H^{2}}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}Q_{H}(\mathsf{v}).

Finally applying
[
5
, Proposition 2.5 & 2.6]
to
F_{i}\otimes D_{i}^{-1}
imply

(A.67)

(-1)^{i+1}m_{i}\ \leq\ \frac{2}{3}\beta_{i}.H\left(\frac{\beta_{i}.H}{H^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\right).

Conversely, take two classes
\mathsf{v}_{i}=(-1)^{i}e^{D_{i}}(1,0,-\beta_{i},-m_{i})
for
i=1,2
satisfying
\mathsf{v}_{1}+\mathsf{v}_{2}=\mathsf{v}
and conditions (
A.22
) and (
A.23
). Then we have

(A.68)

\left|\frac{D_{1}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}-b_{1}\right|<1,\qquad\left|\frac{D_{2}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}-b_{2}\right|<1

and
\frac{D_{2}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}-\frac{D_{1}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}=r
. The Hodge index theorem implies

(A.69)

\frac{D_{i}^{2}.H}{2H^{3}}-\frac{\beta_{i}.H}{H^{3}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{D_{i}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}\right)^{2},

thus
\varpi(E_{i})
lies outside
U
and above or on
\ell_{f}
by (
A.23
). Since by our assumption on
Q_{H}(\mathsf{v})
, we have
b_{2}-b_{1}>\max\{r-1,\ \frac{1}{2}\}
, we get

(A.70)

b_{1}-1<\frac{D_{1}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}<b_{1}<b_{2}<\frac{D_{2}.H^{2}}{H^{3}}<b_{2}+1\,.

Then the same argument as above shows that there is no wall for classes
\mathsf{v}_{1}
and
\mathsf{v}_{2}
above or on
\ell_{f}
. Hence large volume limit stable objects of classes
\mathsf{v}_{i}
for
i=1,2
are
\nu_{b,w}
-stable of the same
\nu_{b,w}
-slope along the line
\ell
passing through
\varpi(\mathsf{v}_{i})
for
i=1,2
, which lies above or on
\ell_{f}
. Thus they make a wall for objects of class
\mathsf{v}
.
This completes the proof of the claim.
∎

Appendix B
Other hypergeometric CY threefolds

In this section, we extend the analysis of §
5
to the other hypergeometric CY threefolds, with the exception of
X_{3,2,2}
and
X_{2,2,2,2}
for which the current knowledge of GV invariants is not sufficient yet to uniquely determine (or even guess) the generating series of Abelian D4-D2-D0 indices. In all cases, we assume that the BMT inequality is satisfied.

B.1.
X_{6}

Here we consider the sextic in
\mathds{P}^{4}_{2,1,1,1,1}
, first studied in
[
20
]
. In this case,
\kappa=3
,
n_{1}^{p}=4
and
n_{1}^{c}=0
so the modular form
h_{\mu}
is
uniquely fixed by 4 coefficients. Using the basis (
5.5
), the
generating function proposed in

[
20
]
reads

(B.1)

\begin{split}h_{\mu}=&\,\frac{1}{\eta^{54}}\left[\frac{7E_{4}^{6}+58E_{4}^{3}E_{6}^{2}+7E_{6}^{4}}{216}+\frac{5E_{4}^{4}E_{6}+3E_{4}E_{6}^{3}}{2}\,D\right]\vartheta^{(3)}_{\mu},\end{split}

and has the following expansion:

(B.2)

\begin{split}\hskip-5.69046pth_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{15}{8}}\,\Bigl(\underline{-4+612\mbox{q}}-40392\mbox{q}^{2}+146464860\mbox{q}^{3}+66864926808\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+8105177463840\mbox{q}^{5}+503852503057596\mbox{q}^{6}+20190917119833144\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-5.69046pth_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{15}{8}+\frac{2}{3}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0-15768\mbox{q}}+7621020\mbox{q}^{2}+10739279916\mbox{q}^{3}+1794352963536\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+134622976939812\mbox{q}^{5}+6141990299963544\mbox{q}^{6}+196926747589177416\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

Using (
4.19
), we can rigorously compute and confirm the terms up to (and including) order
\mbox{q}^{9}
and
\mbox{q}^{6}
in these expansions. The term of order
\mbox{q}^{6}
in
h_{0}
can be further verified using Prop.
1
. Furthermore, the terms of order
\mbox{q}^{10}
and
\mbox{q}^{11}
in
h_{0}
as well as
\mbox{q}^{7}

and
\mbox{q}^{8}
in
h_{1}
are reproduced by (
4.19
) with
k=k_{0}-1
. Thus, there is overwhelming evidence that (
B.1
) is correct. While the maximal genus attainable by the standard direct integration method is 48, using modularity, we can predict
GV invariants close to the Castelnuovo bound to arbitrary genus (see Table
6
), and provide sufficiently many boundary conditions in principle
to push the
direct integration method up to genus 63.

B.2.
X_{8}

\hskip-19.91692pt\begin{array}[]{|r|r|rrrrrr|}\hline\cr Q&g_{C}&\delta=0&\delta=1&\delta=2&\delta=3&\delta=4&\delta=5\\

We now consider the octic in
\mathds{P}^{4}_{4,1,1,1,1}
, first studied in
[
20
]
. In this case,
\kappa=2
,
n_{1}^{p}=4
and
n_{1}^{c}=0
so the modular form
h_{\mu}
is
uniquely fixed by 4 coefficients. Using the basis (
5.5
), the
generating function proposed in

[
20
]
reads

(B.3)

\begin{split}h_{\mu}=&\,\frac{1}{\eta^{52}}\left[\frac{103E_{4}^{6}+1472E_{4}^{3}E_{6}^{2}+153E_{6}^{4}}{5184}+\frac{503E_{4}^{4}E_{6}+361E_{4}E_{6}^{3}}{108}\,D\right]\vartheta^{(2)}_{\mu},\end{split}

and has the following expansion:

(B.4)

\begin{split}\hskip-5.69046pth_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{46}{24}}\,\Bigl(\underline{-4+888\mbox{q}}-86140\mbox{q}^{2}+132940136\mbox{q}^{3}+86849300500\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+11756367847000\mbox{q}^{5}+787670811260144\mbox{q}^{6}+33531427162546608\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-5.69046pth_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{46}{24}+\frac{3}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0-59008\mbox{q}}+8615168\mbox{q}^{2}+21430302976\mbox{q}^{3}+3736977423872\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+289181439668352\mbox{q}^{5}+13588569634434304\mbox{q}^{6}+448400041603851008\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

Using (
4.19
), we can rigorously compute and confirm the terms up to (and including) order
\mbox{q}^{9}
and
\mbox{q}^{7}
in these expansions. The term of order
\mbox{q}^{4}
in
h_{0}
can be further verified using Prop.
1
. The terms of order
\mbox{q}^{10}
,
\mbox{q}^{11}
in
h_{0}

as well as
\mbox{q}^{8}
,
\mbox{q}^{9}
and
\mbox{q}^{10}
in
h_{1}
are reproduced by (
4.19
) with
k=k_{0}-1
. Thus, there is overwhelming evidence that (
B.3
) is correct. While the maximal genus attainable by the standard direct integration method is 60, using modularity, we can predict
GV invariants close to the Castelnuovo bound to arbitrary genus (see Table
7
), and provide sufficiently many boundary conditions in principle
to push the
direct integration method up to genus 80.

B.3.
X_{4,3}

We now consider the complete intersection of degree
(4,3)
in
\mathds{P}^{5}_{2,1,1,1,1,1}
, In this case,
\kappa=6
,
n_{1}^{p}=9
and
n_{1}^{c}=0
so the modular form
h_{\mu}
is
uniquely fixed by 9 coefficients. This model was
first considered in
[
31
]
, assuming the naive Ansatz (
4.8
) for the polar terms. Unfortunately, with the GV invariants being known only up to genus 20 using direct integration, Eq. (
4.19
) only allows to determine 3 polar coefficients:

(B.5)

\begin{split}h_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{5-624\mbox{q}+\tfrac{1}{21}\operatorname{PT}(18,-34)\mbox{q}^{2}}-\tfrac{1}{20}\operatorname{PT}(18,-33)\mbox{q}^{3}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{7}{12}}\,\Bigl(\underline{-\tfrac{1}{12}\operatorname{PT}(13,-20)-\tfrac{1}{24}\operatorname{PT}(19,-38)\mbox{q}}+\tfrac{1}{23}\operatorname{PT}(19,-37)\mbox{q}^{2}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{2}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{1}{3}}\,\Bigl(\underline{-\tfrac{1}{14}\operatorname{PT}(14,-23)+\tfrac{1}{13}\operatorname{PT}(14,-22)\mbox{q}}-\tfrac{1}{26}\operatorname{PT}(20,-41)\mbox{q}^{2}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{3}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{1}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+\tfrac{1}{15}\operatorname{PT}(15,-25)\mbox{q}}-\tfrac{1}{14}\operatorname{PT}(15,-24)\mbox{q}^{2}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

Despite this discouraging result, one can proceed assuming that
for some coefficients
Eq. (
4.19
) still holds for
k=k_{0}-1
.
This assumption will be justified
a posteriori
by matching
the predictions of (
4.19
) and modularity for many more coefficients.
For this choice of the spectral flow parameter, one finds

(B.6)

\begin{split}h_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{2-234\mbox{q}+35415\mbox{q}^{2}}+19018272\mbox{q}^{3}+\tfrac{523497643503}{7}\,\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{7}{12}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+\left(5832+\tfrac{40}{11}{{\rm GV}}^{(21)}_{13}\right)\mbox{q}}-\left(544320+78{{\rm GV}}^{(21)}_{13}\right)\mbox{q}^{2}\\
&\,+\left(3919923072+\tfrac{9880}{9}\,{{\rm GV}}^{(21)}_{13}\right)\mbox{q}^{3}+\left(2506521907872-\tfrac{45695}{4}\,{{\rm GV}}^{(21)}_{13}\right)\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{1}{3}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+0\mbox{q}}-\tfrac{1}{12}\left({{\rm GV}}^{(22)}_{14}+44{{\rm GV}}^{(23)}_{14}+1035{{\rm GV}}^{(24)}_{14}\right)\mbox{q}^{2}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{1}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+0\mbox{q}}+0\mbox{q}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{13}\operatorname{PT}(15,-23)\mbox{q}^{3}+\dots\Bigr),\end{split}

where we expressed the result in terms of GV invariants and put question marks to emphasize
that these expansions need not be correct. For example, the first two terms
in
h_{0}
clearly disagree with the rigorous result (
B.5
).
Nonetheless, let us assume that all other polar terms, except the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q})
term in
h_{2}
,
are correctly computed by (
B.6
).
Comparing with (
B.5
), this implies the vanishing of

\operatorname{PT}(13,-20)={{\rm GV}}^{(21)}_{13}
and
\operatorname{PT}(14,-23)={{\rm GV}}^{(24)}_{14}
, which allows to further simplify (
B.6
).
In particular, all polar terms, except the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q})
term in
h_{2}
, are now fixed.

To get a sufficient number of conditions to fix the modular form, let us further assume that the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q}^{3})
term in
h_{0}
is
also correctly computed by (
B.6
). This assumption provides the missing condition and allows to find a unique
modular form matching all coefficients

(B.7)

\begin{split}h_{\mu}=&\,\frac{1}{\eta^{72}}\left[\frac{19161576E_{4}^{7}E_{6}-86969808E_{4}^{4}E_{6}^{3}-36701208E_{4}E_{6}^{5}}{17199267840}\right.\\
&\,+\frac{29888136E_{4}^{8}+147874032E_{4}^{5}E_{6}^{2}+16326792E_{4}^{2}E_{6}^{4}}{716636160}\,D\\
&\,-\frac{4751784E_{4}^{6}E_{6}+9532080E_{4}^{3}E_{6}^{3}+646056E_{6}^{5}}{5971968}\,D^{2}\\
&\,\left.-\frac{1686312E_{4}^{7}+10686384E_{4}^{4}E_{6}^{2}+2557224E_{4}E_{6}^{4}}{1244160}\,D^{3}\right]\vartheta^{(6)}_{\mu},\end{split}

with the following expansion:

(B.8)

\begin{split}h_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{5-624\mbox{q}+\underline{35415}\mbox{q}^{2}}+19018272\mbox{q}^{3}+74785378407\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+23744184704784\mbox{q}^{5}+2912626940217084\mbox{q}^{6}+201892603398250080\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{7}{12}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+5832\mbox{q}}-544320\mbox{q}^{2}+3919923072\mbox{q}^{3}+2506521907872\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+426826821029328\mbox{q}^{5}+36510169956413184\mbox{q}^{6}+1975570599744644544\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{2}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{1}{3}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+81\mbox{q}}-\dotuline{455544\mbox{q}^{2}}+418794867\mbox{q}^{3}+589406293224\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+127700521014312\mbox{q}^{5}+12611391702441624\mbox{q}^{6}+754527616229888955\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr),\\
h_{3}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{1}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+0\mbox{q}}-\dotuline{322496\mbox{q}^{2}}+154768800\mbox{q}^{3}+356674019472\mbox{q}^{4}+84550767361152\mbox{q}^{5}\\
&\,+8789804684886144\mbox{q}^{6}+544775594940872640\mbox{q}^{7}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

Remarkably, we find that all terms up to
\mbox{q}^{4}
in
h_{1}
turn out to coincide with those in
(
B.6
), which provides strong support for the above assumptions leading to (
B.7
).
Furthermore, only the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q}^{2})
term in
h_{0}
differs from the value
35334
given by the naive ansatz (
4.8
),
while all other polar terms as well as the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q}^{2})
coefficients in
h_{2}
and
h_{3}
perfectly match (
4.8
).

We can apply a similar procedure to provide additional constraints on GV invariants and additional checks
on the modular function (
B.7
). First, taking into account that

\operatorname{PT}(14,-22)={{\rm GV}}^{(23)}_{14}+46{{\rm GV}}^{(24)}_{14}={{\rm GV}}^{(23)}_{14}
and matching the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q})
term in
h_{2}
between (
B.5
)
and (
B.8
), one obtains
{{\rm GV}}^{(23)}_{14}=1053
.
To get a constraint at genus 22, we further assume that the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q}^{2})
term in
h_{2}
is correctly captured by (
B.6
).
Comparing it with (
B.8
) and taking into account the previous findings for GV invariants,
one gets
{{\rm GV}}^{(22)}_{14}=5420196
. With all these constraints, it is possible to compute GV invariants up to genus 23
and check that the coefficients of
\mbox{q}^{3}
and
\mbox{q}^{4}
in
h_{2}
computed using (
4.19
)
with
k=k_{0}-1
match those in (
B.8
), which can be considered as a verification
of the above assumption.

To go to even higher genus, the predictions of modularity based on the rigorous use of (
4.19
)
are again insufficient because GV invariants at genus 24 turn out to depend on unknown PT invariants.
In particular, it can be shown that
{{\rm GV}}^{(24)}_{15}=\operatorname{PT}(15,-23)-216717312
.
Fortunately, we can apply the same trick as above: let us assume that the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q}^{3})
term
in
h_{3}
is computed correctly by (
B.6
). This fixes the required PT invariant and
gives
{{\rm GV}}^{(24)}_{15}=1795277088
. As a result, the direct integration method can be pushed up to genus 24, while the maximal genus attainable by the standard direct integration method is only 20.
One can also check that the coefficients of
\mbox{q}^{4}
and
\mbox{q}^{5}
in
h_{3}
computed using (
4.19
)
with
k=k_{0}-1
match those in (
B.8
), which supports the above assumption.
Finally, using modularity, we can predict GV invariants close to the Castelnuovo bound to arbitrary genus (see Table
8
).

We now consider the complete intersection of degree
(6,4)
in
\mathds{P}^{5}_{3,2,2,1,1,1}
, In this case,
\kappa=2
,
n_{1}^{p}=3
and
n_{1}^{c}=0
so the modular form
h_{\mu}
is uniquely fixed by 3 coefficients. This model was
first considered in
[
31
]
, assuming the naive Ansatz (
4.8
) for the polar terms. Using GV invariants up to genus 14, Eq. (
4.19
) predicts

(B.9)

\begin{split}\hskip-17.07182pth_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{34}{24}}\,\Bigl(\underline{3-\underline{304}\mbox{q}}+\tfrac{1}{13}\operatorname{PT}(8,-18)\mbox{q}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{12}\operatorname{PT}(8,-17)\mbox{q}^{3}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-17.07182pth_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{34}{24}+\frac{3}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{-16}-\tfrac{1}{10}\operatorname{PT}(7,-14)\mbox{q}+\tfrac{1}{9}\left(\operatorname{PT}(7,-13)+192\right)\mbox{q}^{2}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

In particular, the polar part of
h_{0}
differs from the value

3-312\mbox{q}
predicted by the naive Ansatz (
4.8
).
There is a unique modular form that matches these polar terms, namely

(B.10)

\begin{split}h_{\mu}=&\,\frac{1}{\eta^{40}}\left[-\frac{85E_{4}^{3}E_{6}+23E_{6}^{3}}{432}-\frac{13E_{4}^{4}+23E_{4}E_{6}^{2}}{6}\,D\right]\vartheta^{(2)}_{\mu},\end{split}

with the following expansion:

(B.11)

\begin{split}\hskip-17.07182pth_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{34}{24}}\,\Bigl(\underline{3-\underline{304}\mbox{q}}+270431\mbox{q}^{2}+133585104\mbox{q}^{3}+12401092398\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-17.07182pth_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{34}{24}+\frac{3}{4}}\,\Bigl(\underline{-16}+32352\mbox{q}+36578048\mbox{q}^{2}+4364892672\mbox{q}^{3}+226014399392\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

The term of order
q
in
h_{1}
is correctly reproduced by (
4.19
) with
k=k_{0}-1
.
Assuming that (
B.19
) is correct, one can produce additional boundary conditions for the direct integration method (see Table
9
), allowing to reach genus 17, beyond the genus 14 available by standard methods.
Note that to get a boundary condition at genus 17, one uses the fact the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q}^{4})
coefficient in
h_{0}
is subject to Prop.
1
.

B.5.
X_{3,3}

Next, we consider the bicubic in
\mathds{P}^{5}
, first studied in
[
20
]
. In this case,
\kappa=9
,
n_{1}^{p}=14
and
n_{1}^{c}=1
(as first noted in
[
52
]
) so the modular form
h_{\mu}
is
uniquely fixed by 13 coefficients. Using the basis (
5.5
), the
generating function proposed in

[
20
]
reads

(B.12)

\begin{split}h_{\mu}=&\,\frac{1}{\eta^{90}}\left[\frac{47723E_{4}^{9}E_{6}+25095E_{4}^{6}E_{6}^{3}-68943E_{4}^{3}E_{6}^{5}-3875E_{6}^{7}}{107495424}\right.\\
&\,+\frac{289326E_{4}^{10}+415189E_{4}^{7}E_{6}^{2}-3458324E_{4}^{4}E_{6}^{4}-729839E_{4}E_{6}^{6}}{334430208}\,D\\
&\,+\frac{2261629E_{4}^{8}E_{6}+3219046E_{4}^{5}E_{6}^{3}-6371E_{4}^{2}E_{6}^{5}}{30965760}\,D^{2}\\
&\,-\frac{94271E_{4}^{9}+1496733E_{4}^{6}E_{6}^{2}+1342665E_{4}^{3}E_{6}^{4}+52315E_{6}^{6}}{5160960}\,D^{3}\\
&\,\left.-\frac{162167E_{4}^{7}E_{6}+300338E_{4}^{4}E_{6}^{3}+35159E_{4}E_{6}^{5}}{286720}\,D^{4}\right]\vartheta^{(9)}_{\mu},\end{split}

and has the following expansion

(B.13)

\begin{split}\hskip-11.38092pth_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{63}{24}}\,\Bigl(-\underline{6+720\mbox{q}-40032\mbox{q}^{2}}-678474\mbox{q}^{3}+30885198768\mbox{q}^{4}+35708825468142\mbox{q}^{5}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-11.38092pth_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{63}{24}+\frac{5}{9}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0-4212\mbox{q}+448578\mbox{q}^{2}}+374980104\mbox{q}^{3}+2020724648442\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-11.38092pth_{2}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{63}{24}+\frac{2}{9}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+0\mbox{q}+158436\mbox{q}^{2}}-12471246\mbox{q}^{3}+174600085086\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-11.38092pth_{3}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{63}{24}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+0\mbox{q}+10206\mbox{q}^{2}}-\dotuline{13828428\mbox{q}^{3}}+24425287884\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-11.38092pth_{4}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{63}{24}+\frac{8}{9}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+0\mbox{q}}-\dotuline{11040786\mbox{q}^{2}}+6769752552\mbox{q}^{3}+17629606262268\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

Unfortunately, with GV invariants being known up to genus 29, Eq. (
4.19
) only allows to confirm the coefficients
-6+720\mbox{q}
in
h_{0}
and
0\,\mbox{q}^{0}
in
h_{3}
. Applying (
4.19
) with
k=k_{0}-1
, we find evidence that
the coefficients of all terms up to
\mbox{q}^{5}
in
h_{0}
and all the vanishing coefficients in other components are indeed correct.
Moreover, the
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q}^{3})
coefficient in
h_{3}
and
\mathcal{O}(\mbox{q}^{2})
coefficient in
h_{4}
as well as all polar terms turn out to agree with the ansatz (
4.8
).
In addition, we observe that the coefficients
-4212
in
h_{1}
and 10206 in
h_{3}
are given by
\frac{1}{5}\operatorname{PT}(10,-9)
and
-\frac{1}{6}\operatorname{PT}(12,-12)
, even though the corresponding values of
(Q,m)
do not satisfy the optimality conditions.
Thus, there is strong evidence that (
B.13
) is correct.

B.6.
X_{4,4}

We now consider the complete intersection of degree
(4,4)
in
\mathds{P}^{5}_{2,2,1,1,1}
, In this case,
\kappa=4
,
n_{1}^{p}=6
and
n_{1}^{c}=1
so the modular form
h_{\mu}
is uniquely fixed by 5 coefficients. This model was
first considered in
[
31
]
, assuming the naive Ansatz (
4.8
) for the polar terms. Using GV invariants up to genus 26, Eq. (
4.19
) predicts

(B.14)

\begin{split}\hskip-5.69046pth_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{44}{24}}\,\Bigl(\underline{-4+432\mbox{q}}-10032\mbox{q}^{2}+148611456\mbox{q}^{3}-\tfrac{1}{24}\operatorname{PT}(16,-36)_{4}\mbox{q}^{4}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-5.69046pth_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{44}{24}+\frac{5}{8}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0+\tfrac{1}{17}\operatorname{PT}(13,-26)\mbox{q}}-\tfrac{1}{16}\operatorname{PT}(13,-25)\mbox{q}^{2}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-5.69046pth_{2}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{44}{24}+\frac{1}{2}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0-2816\mbox{q}}+\tfrac{1}{19}\operatorname{PT}(14,-29)\mbox{q}^{2}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

This is sufficient information to fully determine the generating series:

(B.15)

\begin{split}h_{\mu}=&\,\frac{1}{\eta^{56}}\left[\frac{319E_{4}^{5}E_{6}+113E_{4}^{2}E_{6}^{3}}{11664}-\frac{146E_{4}^{6}+1025E_{4}^{3}E_{6}^{2}+125E_{6}^{4}}{972}\,D\right.\\
&\,\left.-\frac{566E_{4}^{4}E_{6}+298E_{4}E_{6}^{3}}{81}\,D^{2}\right]\vartheta^{(4)}_{\mu},\end{split}

with the following expansion:

(B.16)

\begin{split}\hskip-5.69046pth_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{44}{24}}\,\Bigl(\underline{-4+432\mbox{q}}-10032\mbox{q}^{2}+148611456\mbox{q}^{3}+53495321332\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+5858228664240\mbox{q}^{5}+338470263518000\mbox{q}^{6}++12773210724578176\mbox{q}^{7}\\
&\,+352882974651781356\mbox{q}^{8}\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-5.69046pth_{1}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{44}{24}+\frac{5}{8}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0-7424\mbox{q}}+7488256\mbox{q}^{2}+7149513728\mbox{q}^{3}+1104027086592\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+78370863237632\mbox{q}^{5}+3411805769659904\mbox{q}^{6}+\dots\Bigr),\\
\hskip-5.69046pth_{2}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{44}{24}+\frac{1}{2}}\,\Bigl(\underline{0-2816\mbox{q}}+2167680\mbox{q}^{2}+3503031296\mbox{q}^{3}+619015800576\mbox{q}^{4}\\
&\,+47430532268544\mbox{q}^{5}+2174342476769792\mbox{q}^{6}+\dots\Bigr).\end{split}

In particular, the polar part agrees with the naive Ansatz (
4.8
) that was assumed in
[
31
]
.
Assuming that (
B.15
) is correct, one can produce additional boundary conditions for the direct integration method (see Table
11
) allowing to reach genus 32.
Furthermore, the term of order
\mbox{q}^{8}
in
h_{0}
is subject to Prop.
1
and provides an additional boundary condition at genus 33 that allows to push the direct integration
up to genus 34.
With this new data, we can further check
that (
B.16
) is consistent up to orders
\mbox{q}^{3}
,
\mbox{q}^{4}
and
\mbox{q}^{5}
with (
4.19
), and even reproduce the coefficients of order
\mbox{q}^{5}
in
h_{1}
and
\mbox{q}^{6}
in
h_{2}
by applying (
4.19
) with
k=k_{0}-1
. Thus, there is overwhelming
evidence that (
B.15
) (first conjectured in
[
31
]
) is indeed correct.

(B.17)

\begin{split}h=-\frac{2E_{4}E_{6}}{\eta^{23}}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{23}{24}}\,\Bigl(-\underline{2}+482\mbox{q}+282410\mbox{q}^{2}+16775192\mbox{q}^{3}+460175332\mbox{q}^{4}\\

) to confirm all terms

. The

.

differs

\chi_{\mathfrak{Y}}(\chi_{D}-1)=-120
, due to the singular curve where the two degree-one homogeneous coordinates vanish simultaneously.

(B.18)

\begin{split}\hskip-11.38092pth_{0}=&\,\mbox{q}^{-\frac{56}{24}}\,\Bigl(\underline{5-1024\mbox{q}+96390\mbox{q}^{2}}+2412544\mbox{q}^{3}+79408559682\mbox{q}^{4}\\

(B.19)

\begin{split}h_{\mu}=&\,\frac{1}{\eta^{68}}\left[-\frac{5(727E_{4}^{8}+3322E_{4}^{5}E_{6}^{2}+1135E_{4}^{2}E_{6}^{4})}{559872}\right.\\

Assuming that (

) is correct, one can produce additional boundary conditions for the direct integration method (see Table
13
), allowing in principle to reach genus 78, beyond the genus 63 available by standard methods.
